At work, my high energy makes me start dozens of projects at once, but I can't stay focused and keep switching tasks, so I never actually finish anything. In my relationships, my extreme irritability has caused major strain because I constantly snap and get angry at people over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty in finishing projects is primarily because you lose interest or get distracted easily, or is it more due to your mood and energy levels shifting rapidly, making it hard to sustain focus on one task?\n\nPatient: It is actually a mix of both. I get distracted incredibly easily by any little noise or movement around me, which makes me constantly abandon what I'm doing to start something new. At the same time, my surging energy and rapidly shifting moods make me feel like I have to jump to the next exciting idea immediately, leaving a trail of unfinished projects behind.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day for you, from waking up to bedtime, and how your energy, mood, and focus change throughout the day, and are there any specific times when you feel more or less in control of your impulses and distractions?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wake up with overflowing energy and immediately start multiple projects at once, like reorganizing my closet while trying to cook breakfast. But within minutes, a tiny noise or a stray thought distracts me, and I abandon everything to jump to a new task. My energy and mood stay intensely high all day long, meaning I never really feel in control of my focus or impulses from morning to night.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli: Attention is immediately drawn to trivial and unimportant stimuli nearby (e.g., noise, movement).\n  - Frequent Task Switching: Unable to finish one task and constantly shifting attention to new things, starting multiple tasks simultaneously.\n  - Inability to Sustain Attention: Unable to focus on any activity for long, with attention constantly being scattered.\n  Duration: 9 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  -     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor, I feel absolutely amazing and so full of life! For the last nine months, I've had this overwhelming surge of energy and happiness where I just can't stop moving or doing things. I feel like I'm on top of the world and there is just so much I want to accomplish!\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling extremely low or depressed, almost the opposite of how you're feeling now, or has it been consistently high energy and happiness for the past nine months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't felt depressed or low at all during this time. For the past nine months, my energy has been consistently high and my mood is usually incredibly elevated, though I do find myself snapping and getting irritated very quickly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these instances where you snap and get irritated - are they in response to specific situations or do they seem to come out of nowhere, and do you also find yourself acting impulsively, such as interrupting others or blurting out thoughts?\n\nPatient: The smallest, most trivial things can suddenly make me snap, and my mood flips instantly from being super happy to incredibly angry. Because of this constant rush of energy, I can't stand waiting and definitely find myself interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any distinct episodes of abnormally elevated or irritable mood that lasted for a shorter period, such as a few days, which were clearly different from your usual mood, or has your mood been consistently elevated with irritability over the nine months?\n\nPatient: It has been completely consistent over the entire nine months. I haven't had short episodes of just a few days; it's just been this continuous, non-stop rush of high energy and irritability every single day.\n\nDoctor: How has this consistent high energy and irritability affected your daily life, relationships, and work or school performance over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: It has completely disrupted my life over the last nine months. At work, my high energy makes me start dozens of projects at once, but I can't stay focused and keep switching tasks, so I never actually finish anything. In my relationships, my extreme irritability has caused major strain because I constantly snap and get angry at people over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty in finishing projects is primarily because you lose interest or get distracted easily, or is it more due to your mood and energy levels shifting rapidly, making it hard to sustain focus on one task?\n\nPatient: It is actually a mix of both. I get distracted incredibly easily by any little noise or movement around me, which makes me constantly abandon what I'm doing to start something new. At the same time, my surging energy and rapidly shifting moods make me feel like I have to jump to the next exciting idea immediately, leaving a trail of unfinished projects behind.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day for you, from waking up to bedtime, and how your energy, mood, and focus change throughout the day, and are there any specific times when you feel more or less in control of your impulses and distractions?\n\nPatient: I wake up with overflowing energy and immediately start multiple projects at once, like reorganizing my closet while trying to cook breakfast. But within minutes, a tiny noise or a stray thought distracts me, and I abandon everything to jump to a new task. My energy and mood stay intensely high all day long, meaning I never really feel in control of my focus or impulses from morning to night.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you needed less sleep than usual, yet still felt rested, or had an increase in self-esteem to the point of grandiosity, and engaged in activities that could have potentially painful consequences, such as excessive spending or risky sexual behavior, as a result of your elevated mood?\n\nPatient: I have definitely felt incredibly elevated and sometimes feel exceptionally powerful or special, like I can achieve absolutely anything. However, I'm not really sure about needing less sleep, and I don't think I've engaged in risky behaviors like excessive spending.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instances where your feeling of being 'exceptionally powerful or special' led you to make decisions that, in hindsight, might not have been the best choices, or where you overlooked potential consequences of your actions due to your elevated self-esteem?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about it that way, to be honest. I'm not sure of any specific bad decisions I've made because of those feelings. To me, it just feels like I am finally capable of doing great things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt invincible, made poor financial decisions, or engaged in reckless behaviors without considering the consequences, and if so, did these episodes coincide with your periods of high energy and elevated mood?"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
